The **Flamingo Revolution** ([Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_language): *Revolucioni i Flamingove*) is a decentralised [social movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_movement) and series of protests primarily against the [Government of Albania](67576.md). The initial protests took place in [Baks-Rrjoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rrjolli) on 2 February 2026 when residents protested against the planned Blue Borgo resort and disputed land ownership.[^cite_note-Rrjoll-start-2Feb-1][^cite_note-VNA-Rrjoll-third-day-47] A major wave of protests followed on 16 May at [Zvërnec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zv%C3%ABrnec), near the [Narta Lagoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagoon_of_Narta), over preparatory works and proposed luxury tourism developments linked to [the Sazan Island and Zvërnec resort project](80220444.md), backed by American investor [Jared Kushner](6191053.md).[^cite_note-ReportTV-Akerni-16May-48][^cite_note-Faktoje-16May-first-protest-49] Violence against protesters near the Zvërnec project site on 30 May triggered further demonstrations in [Tirana](31075.md) the following day. The protests subsequently broadened from Baks-Rrjoll, Zvërnec and Tirana and later spread to other cities in Albania, Kosovo and [Albanian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_diaspora) communities abroad.

As the demonstrations expanded, they evolved into broader opposition against the [government](81051981.md) of prime minister [Edi Rama](277805.md), [corruption](35513678.md), [state capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_capture#Albania), [media capture](15860750.md#Media_capture_and_government_influence), poor governance and [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_Europe_by_country#Albania). On 22 June, protesters formalized five demands: Rama's resignation; the formation of a 12-month non-partisan technical government; constitutional and electoral reforms, including a two-term lifetime limit for the prime minister; the repeal or reversal of disputed laws concerning strategic investments, protected areas, cultural heritage and mountain development; and a new social contract between citizens and the state. Many demonstrations have also criticized [leader of the opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_\(Albania\)) [Sali Berisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sali_Berisha) and the [Democratic Party of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Albania), with slogans portraying both Rama and Berisha as part of the same political establishment.

The main protests have been centred in Tirana, especially around [Skanderbeg Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg_Square), the [prime minister's office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister's_Office_\(Albania\)), [Dëshmorët e Kombit Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%ABshmor%C3%ABt_e_Kombit_Boulevard), [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Albania) and other symbolic public spaces. Daily demonstrations drew tens of thousands of participants, while larger nationwide and diaspora-focused mobilizations were held on 10, 13, 20 June and 4 July. The 20 June protest became the largest demonstration of the movement to date, with Albanian media estimating more than 250,000 participants and describing the crowd as filling much of Dëshmorët e Kombit Boulevard, from [Skanderbeg Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg_Square) to [Mother Teresa Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_Teresa_Square_\(Tirana\)). The movement has been described as part of the [Gen Z protests](77442342.md), with Generation Z and Millennial protesters playing a prominent role, alongside civil society groups, environmental activists, local residents, public figures and diaspora communities.

The [flamingo](165927.md), a wild bird that inhabits the [Vjosa–Narta wetland ecosystem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vjosa%E2%80%93Nart%C3%AB_Protected_Landscape?action=edit\&redlink=1), is the main symbol of the demonstrations, giving rise to the name Flamingo Revolution. Protesters have used flamingo cutouts, inflatable flamingos, national flags and modified protest imagery to represent both environmental protection and wider civic resistance. [Mass national media](15860750.md) such as [Top Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Channel), [Vizion Plus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vizion_Plus) and [Klan TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klan_TV) are widely perceived to have continuously minimized the size of the protests and taken pro-government stances.&#x20;

Rama defended the Sazan Island and Zvërnec resort project as a major foreign investment and rejected allegations of unlawful conduct, suggesting the movement to be influenced by misinformation or foreign interests and later describing it as part of a "hybrid war" used by "enemies of Israel and Albania". The movement has received further criticism from other members of the [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_of_Albania), Berisha and members of the Democratic Party, and other political figures. The movement has received public support from former officials, dissenters of the Socialist Party and several minor opposition parties in Albania, including the [Opportunity Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opportunity_Party_\(Albania\)), [Lëvizja Bashkë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%ABvizja_Bashk%C3%AB) and [Lëvizja Shqipëria Bëhet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%ABvizja_Shqip%C3%ABria_B%C3%ABhet).

## Background

### Sazan Island Resort

Main article: [Sazan Island Resort](80220444.md)



View over the [Narta Lagoon](20113651.md) with [Sazan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sazan) in the background.

[Ivanka Trump](1861441.md) and her husband, [Jared Kushner](6191053.md), first visited Albania on a private trip in July 2021. Trump and Kushner travelled the entire coastline on a yacht owned by [Nathaniel Rothschild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathaniel_Rothschild,_5th_Baron_Rothschild), a friend of theirs.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-kt-51] While out swimming, they encountered the uninhabited, [biodiverse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity) island of [Sazan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sazan), an abandoned military base, and were surprised it had remained undeveloped.[^cite_note-ui-52][^cite_note-it-53] Prime Minister [Edi Rama](277805.md) met them on the yacht during the trip and discussed investment opportunities with Kushner a year later.[^cite_note-it-53][^cite_note-Reuters-2026-06-08-13]

In March 2024, Kushner announced plans to invest in Sazan and the nearby coastal area on the mainland, near the village of [Zvërnec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zv%C3%ABrnec).[^cite_note-BIRN-Investors-54] Environmental groups such as [BirdLife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BirdLife_International) have connected [Kushner's tourism development project](80220444.md) to an amendment to Albania's Protected Areas Law,[^cite_note-Faktoje-55] passed shortly prior.[^cite_note-BIRN-Investors-54] It exempted "structures of excellence, 5 stars or more", and related hospitality activities from restrictions on construction in environmentally protected areas, such as where the project was proposed.[^cite_note-it-53]

The Albanian government granted the Sazan project strategic investment status on 30 December 2025, noting that it would occupy 565 hectares and some 45 hectares would be developed.[^cite_note-56] In 2025, Prime Minister Rama said that Albania "can't afford not to exploit a gift like Sazan" and needed luxury tourism.[^cite_note-ui-52] The land on Sazan was sold in a €1.4-billion deal, which locals and parliamentarians were unaware of until newspapers reported it.[^cite_note-ui-52][^cite_note-Reuters-2026-06-08-13] Afterwards, Albanians began referring to Sazan as "Trump Island" (*Ishulli i Trumpëve*).[^cite_note-ui-52]

Kushner also has plans for a larger second site: the undeveloped coastal stretch of Pishë Poro-Narta within the [Vjosa–Narta Protected Landscape](73303962.md).[^cite_note-it-53] It is home to endangered species such as [monk seals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monk_seal), sea turtles and more than 200 bird species, including flamingos and pelicans.[^cite_note-it-53] Over 10,000 accommodation units are planned.[^cite_note-BIRN-Investors-54][^cite_note-pc-57] Kushner said that he wanted "to create the ideal resort that I'd want to be at with my family and with my friends", and that the government had been very cooperative.[^cite_note-ui-52] According to Rama, the projects together are worth up to €5 billion.[^cite_note-Reuters-2026-06-08-13]

Ivanka Trump is also linked to the project. She mentioned her and Kushner's plans on podcasts,[^cite_note-ui-52] bringing them to wider attention.[^cite_note-it-53] Her description of Sazan as their "private island" angered Albanians.[^cite_note-kt-51] The project became the subject of public debate. Opponents, including residents and environmental organizations, expressed concerns regarding the potential environmental impact of the development and raised questions concerning land ownership within the project area.[^cite_note-Reporter-31] Representatives of the project stated that the development was being pursued in accordance with Albanian law and applicable planning procedures.[^cite_note-Panorama-58]

### Corruption and state capture

Main articles: [Corruption in Albania](35513678.md) and [State capture § Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_capture#Albania)

The resort controversy developed against a broader background of public concern over corruption, state capture and the relationship between political power, private business interests and public institutions in Albania. [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) classified Albania as "Partly Free" in its 2025 report and stated that corruption and bribery remained major problems despite reforms in the justice system.[^cite_note-FreedomHouse2025-59] The [European Commission](9974.md)'s 2025 Albania report stated that the institutional framework for fighting corruption was in place, but that preventive measures still needed improvement and that the [state police](6225413.md) remained highly vulnerable to corruption, while also noting positive results by [SPAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Structure_against_Corruption_and_Organized_Crime) in high-level corruption cases.[^cite_note-60]

Commentators connected the protests to wider grievances about oligarchic influence, opaque public decision-making and the use of laws for private or politically connected interests. BiEPAG described the Flamingo Revolution as exposing Albania's "systemic weaknesses", including private security violence, opaque legal amendments and a captured media landscape.[^cite_note-BIEPAG-Flamingo-4] Other analysis described the protests as a channel for wider anger at political elites accused of abusing power and institutions, capturing parts of the media landscape and instrumentalising the law for partisan purposes.[^cite_note-InstitutJacquesDelors-61]

Several high-profile corruption and procurement cases during Rama's premiership contributed to the broader climate in which the protests developed. In the [Albanian incinerators scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_incinerators_scandal), former environment minister [Lefter Koka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lefter_Koka) was sentenced to prison on corruption, abuse-of-power and money-laundering charges related to waste-incinerator contracts, while former deputy prime minister [Arben Ahmetaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arben_Ahmetaj) was charged in connection with the same affair and left Albania; Ahmetaj denied wrongdoing.[^cite_note-Reuters-incinerators-2025-62][^cite_note-AP-Koka-incinerator-63][^cite_note-TI-Ahmetaj-incinerators-64] The [AKSHI public procurement scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AKSHI_public_procurement_scandal) also became part of wider criticism of state contracting, after SPAK investigated alleged manipulation of public tenders at the [National Agency for Information Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Agency_for_Information_Society_\(Albania\)) (AKSHI), an agency under the authority of the Prime Minister's office that managed major digital-infrastructure and [eAlbania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EAlbania) contracts.[^cite_note-TiranaTimes-AKSHI-65][^cite_note-ADN-AKSHI-assets-66]

### Democratic backsliding and concentration of power

Main article: [Democratic backsliding in Europe by country § Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_Europe_by_country#Albania)

The protests also took place in the context of concerns about democratic backsliding, concentration of power and long-term political polarisation. The [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_of_Albania), led by [Edi Rama](277805.md), has governed Albania since 2013 and won a fourth consecutive parliamentary election in 2025.[^cite_note-67] Freedom House described Albanian politics as competitive but highly polarised, with political parties often organised around leading personalities.[^cite_note-FreedomHouse2025-59]

Analysts argued that the dominance of the Socialist Party under Rama and the Democratic Party under [Sali Berisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sali_Berisha) had left limited space for credible political alternatives and had contributed to a toxic political environment. In this interpretation, the protests expressed not only opposition to the resort project, but also fatigue with both the government and the established opposition. The chants "Rama to prison, Berisha to prison" ("Rama në burg, Berisha në burg!") reflected this broader rejection of the two dominant political camps. Criticism of Berisha was also reinforced by the Rrjoll dispute, where resort developer Bashkim Ulaj had been described in Albanian media as close to the Berisha family and residents publicly questioned Berisha's lack of support for their protest.[^cite_note-Shqiptarja-Berisha-Ulaj-2024-68][^cite_note-Prapaskena-Rrjoll-Berisha-69] Rama's governing style was also described by critics as increasingly associated with concentrated decision-making and reduced space for parliamentary dialogue or civil society consultation.[^cite_note-InstitutJacquesDelors-61]

Election-related controversies under Rama also formed part of the protesters' wider criticism of state institutions and political power. In 2019, *[Bild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bild)* published audio recordings connected to the 2017 parliamentary elections, including conversations involving Socialist officials and local power brokers that opposition parties and critics described as evidence of vote-buying, intimidation and links between politics and organised crime.[^cite_note-70] The [Patronageists scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patronageists_scandal?action=edit\&redlink=1) in 2021 raised further concerns about the use of personal data and party-linked voter monitoring, after *Lapsi.al* reported that a database allegedly connected to the Socialist Party contained personal information and political-preference notes for around 910,000 voters or residents of Tirana.[^cite_note-TI-Patronageists-2021-71][^cite_note-CPJ-Lapsi-2021-72] The OSCE/ODIHR final report on the 2025 parliamentary election later stated that the vote was competitive and professionally managed, but that contestants did not enjoy a level playing field and cited allegations of intimidation, misuse of public resources, pressure on public employees and vote-buying.[^cite_note-ODIHR-2025-final-73]

Concerns about the separation of powers also grew in 2026 after Rama announced plans for legislation that would prevent courts from suspending ministers under criminal investigation. [Reuters](18998750.md) reported that opposition parties said the proposal would undermine judicial independence and protect Rama's allies, while Rama accused the judiciary of overreach.[^cite_note-Reuters-judiciary-2026-74] The proposal followed corruption proceedings against senior government figures, including Deputy Prime Minister and Infrastructure Minister [Belinda Balluku](65301641.md), who denied allegations over the alleged manipulation of public tenders.[^cite_note-Reuters-Balluku-court-75]

### Media capture and government influence

Main article: [Mass media in Albania § Media capture and government influence](15860750.md#Media_capture_and_government_influence)

Concerns over [media capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_capture) and political influence over public information also formed part of the background to the protests. The 2026 [World Press Freedom Index](8264708.md), issued by [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md), ranked Albania 83rd out of 180 countries, stating that [press freedom](105323.md) and media independence were threatened by [conflicts of interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflicts_of_interest) between business and politics, a flawed legal framework and partisan regulation. It also stated that journalists who were critical of the authorities were often subjected to discrediting attacks, that access to state-held information was difficult, and that [self-censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-censorship) was widespread.[^cite_note-76]

[Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International) EU similarly noted concerns about concentration of mass media ownership, lack of transparency in mass media ownership, and the independence of public service media, while also reporting an increase in attacks against journalists in 2025.[^cite_note-77]

During the early phase of the protests, media capture became one of the issues raised by commentators and activists. BiEPAG argued that several mainstream outlets had ignored live coverage or spread disinformation about the protests, including by amplifying claims that the demonstrations were externally sponsored or motivated by foreign interests.[^cite_note-BIEPAG-Flamingo-4] Reporter.al also described the use of artificial-intelligence-generated images and police-camera material against the protest narrative, linking the information campaign to attempts to discredit the movement.[^cite_note-Reporter-IA-police-cameras-78] The reliance of protesters on social media, livestreams, memes and diaspora networks was therefore partly framed as a response to distrust of traditional media and perceived government influence over national broadcasters and major private outlets.[^cite_note-BIEPAG-Flamingo-4]

Earlier media-freedom disputes under Rama also shaped the protest movement's distrust of mainstream media and official narratives. In 2019, Rama's government proposed changes to Albania's media legislation, known by critics as the "anti-defamation" package, which would have allowed the Audiovisual Media Authority to fine online media outlets and order the removal or blocking of content. Albanian and international media organisations criticised the draft laws as a threat to free expression and online journalism.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80] The Albanian Ombudsman said the draft laws did not meet international standards, while the Venice Commission later identified several flaws and recommended changes intended to protect freedom of expression.[^cite_note-81]

Rama has also clashed publicly with [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) over assessments of media freedom in Albania. After Albania's fall in the World Press Freedom Index, Rama dismissed parts of the organisation's criticism as "lies" and "fantasies". Reporters Without Borders responded by criticising his treatment of journalists and by pointing to political attacks and pressure on critical reporters.[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83]

### Beginning of the protests

Residents of Zvërnec were not informed about the development and became aware almost exclusively through media reports.[^cite_note-BIRN-Investors-54][^cite_note-ui-52] At the end of April 2026, the company Zvërnec South Adriatic Development fenced off part of the protected coastal area of Zvërnec-Portonovo as part of the preparations using a disputed work permit.[^cite_note-Panorama-58][^cite_note-Reporter-IA-police-cameras-78] Residents, such as village headman Kostaq Konomi, said that the beach contained ancestral properties claimed within cadastral zone 3951; for two decades, locals have been involved in legal land disputes with businessmen such as Artur Shehu.[^cite_note-BIRN-Investors-54]

Reporter.al, the Albanian outlet of the [Balkan Investigative Reporting Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Investigative_Reporting_Network), reported that Zvërnec South Adriatic Development was established in August 2024 and owned by Dutch Trust Management B.V, a Dutch trust, obscuring the owners' identities. The company is linked through a network of shell companies to the Sazan Island project and to Qatari investor brothers [Ramez Al-Khayyat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramez_Al-Khayyat) and Mohamad Al-Khayyat. The outlet also reported that the company had secured two unpublished development permits from the National Territorial Council for tourist resorts in Zvërnec, which had not been officially published, and that later preparatory fencing and access-road works were carried out after company representatives presented inspectors with an AZHT document that Reporter.al said was still awaiting formal approval.[^cite_note-BIRN-Investors-54][^cite_note-BIRN-Controversy-84] During the 2026 protests, Rama confirmed Kushner and Trump's involvement but also said that there was a wider group of investors and architects from Denmark, France, Greece, Japan, and Turkey.[^cite_note-it-53]

On 30 May 2026, activists and local residents were assaulted by private security personnel. Police were present but did not intervene as a demonstrator was dragged inside the fenced area.[^cite_note-Reporter-IA-police-cameras-78] These events expanded the local dispute into mass protests in Tirana.[^cite_note-85]

### Symbols



View of flamingos flying over the Narta Lagoon

The [flamingo](165927.md) became a symbol of the protests, which were described by several media outlets as the "Flamingo Revolution".[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-france24.com-87][^cite_note-politico.eu-88] [Greater flamingos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_flamingo) (*Phoenicopterus roseus*) are among the species that inhabit the protected [Vjosa–Narta wetland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vjosa%E2%80%93Nart%C3%AB_Protected_Landscape?action=edit\&redlink=1) ecosystem.[^cite_note-pc-57][^cite_note-89][^cite_note-AP-3June-90] During demonstrations, protesters carried flamingo cutouts and inflatable flamingos.[^cite_note-Reuters-5June-91][^cite_note-AP-3June-90]

Some protest imagery replaced the eagle on the [flag of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Albania) with flamingos and changed the red background to blue, a reference to the sea and wetland ecosystem.[^cite_note-92] [BalkanWeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BalkanWeb) described the flamingo as a civic protest symbol that was not directly associated with any existing Albanian political party.[^cite_note-93]

The [Straw Hats' Jolly Roger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straw_Hats'_Jolly_Roger) flag from the manga series *[One Piece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Piece)* was also reported to have appeared at the protests.[^cite_note-TPZ-94] The flag has been used in other youth-led protest movements internationally as a symbol of resistance to authority.[^cite_note-95] Some sources have described the demonstrations as [Gen Z protests](77442342.md) due to [Generation Z](4015968.md)'s prominent role in their organization and symbolism.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-TPZ-94]

## Demands

The movement's stated demands combined political, constitutional, economic and environmental objectives. The five-demand document was drafted by the **Protest Coordinating Group** following proposals received from citizens during more than three weeks of demonstrations.[^cite_note-Lapsi-2026-06-22-demands-14][^cite_note-Syri-2026-06-22-demands-15] It was read publicly for the first time on 22 June by Dritan Goxhaj, a former Albanian army officer described by Albanian media as a former [Kosovo Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_Liberation_Army) fighter.[^cite_note-Lapsi-Goxhaj-demands-authorship-97][^cite_note-Lapsi-2026-06-22-demands-14][^cite_note-Syri-2026-06-22-demands-15][^cite_note-VNA-Goxhaj-profile-98] The coordinating group subsequently sent the document in a formal letter to Western ambassadors and heads of diplomatic missions accredited in Albania. The letter was signed on behalf of the group by Entela Koja, Arben Kola, Megan Balaj, Edviol Kulluri, Luçjana Kokaj, Gentjan Progni, Gjon Ndoj, Etleva Merko, Everest Bardhi, Përparim Gjeka, Mevlan Mata, Ilir Xhemalaj and Marjen Rama.[^cite_note-Prapaskena-coordinating-group-99][^cite_note-OraNews-coordinating-group-100][^cite_note-Shqiptarja-coordinating-group-101]

1. The non-negotiable resignation of Prime Minister [Edi Rama](277805.md) and the entire [government cabinet](81051981.md).

2. The establishment of a non-partisan technical transitional government with a clearly defined 12-month mandate.

3. The drafting of a new constitution guaranteeing full equality of citizens before the law, to be approved through a national referendum. The proposed constitutional reform would include:&#x20;

   1. Amendment of the [Electoral Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Albania).

   2. Amendment of legislation governing the financing of political parties and organizations.

   3. A lifetime limit of no more than two full or partial terms for any individual serving as [prime minister](259136.md).

4. The continued pursuit of the movement's initial economic and environmental demands, including:&#x20;

   1. Repeal of the amendments to the law on protected areas.

   2. Repeal of the amendments to the law on cultural heritage.

   3. Repeal of the legislative package known as the "Mountain Package".

   4. Repeal of the status and legal framework governing strategic investments.

5. The establishment of a new social contract between citizens and the state, to be drafted through consultations with intellectuals, technical experts and non-partisan citizens proposed directly by participants in the protest square.

   [^cite_note-Lapsi-2026-06-22-demands-14]

   [^cite_note-Syri-2026-06-22-demands-15]

## Protests

### February 2026: Rrjoll protests

On 2 February 2026, residents of [Baks-Rrjoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rrjolli) in the Velipojë area began a renewed protest wave against the Blue Borgo tourism development after construction machinery returned to the site. Residents said that land claimed by local families was being used for the project while ownership disputes remained unresolved.[^cite_note-Rrjoll-start-2Feb-1] By 4 February, Albanian media described the demonstrations as being in their third consecutive day, with clashes between residents and police and representatives of roughly 60 families calling for the works to stop.[^cite_note-VNA-Rrjoll-third-day-47]

The Rrjoll dispute centred on a resort project associated with businessman [Bashkim Ulaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bashkim_Ulaj?action=edit\&redlink=1) and his company Gener 2. The protests broadened beyond the construction site, including a demonstration outside [SPAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Structure_against_Corruption_and_Organized_Crime) and calls for investigation of alleged property-document irregularities.[^cite_note-Rrjoll-SPAK-5Feb-102]

The Rrjoll protests also became a point of criticism toward the established opposition. Local coverage questioned the absence of Democratic Party representatives from the demonstrations, and one resident publicly criticized [Sali Berisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sali_Berisha) for not supporting the protest against Ulaj's resort.[^cite_note-ShkodraWeb-Rrjoll-PD-103][^cite_note-Prapaskena-Rrjoll-Berisha-69] Albanian media have also described Ulaj as a businessman or publisher close to the Berisha family; this relationship formed part of protesters' criticism of Berisha's position, although such descriptions do not establish that Berisha had a formal role in the Rrjoll development.[^cite_note-Shqiptarja-Berisha-Ulaj-2024-68]

### May 2026: Zvërnec and the national trigger

On 16 May, the first protest against the project was held at the [Akërni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ak%C3%ABrni?action=edit\&redlink=1) construction site in the [Pishë Poro–Narta area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vjosa-Narta_Protected_Landscape). Local residents and environmental activists marched to the fenced site, removed part of its entrance and entered the construction area, arguing that the works threatened a protected landscape and that information about the development had not been made public. The [National Inspectorate for Territorial Protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Inspectorate_for_Territorial_Protection_\(Albania\)) said that the activity consisted of fencing and preparatory work for access roads on privately registered land and was covered by a development permit and an environmental-impact report.[^cite_note-ReportTV-Akerni-16May-48][^cite_note-Faktoje-16May-first-protest-49]

On 23 May, local residents and environmental activists gathered near the [Narta Lagoon](20113651.md) to protest the barbed-wire fencing of Pishë Poro-Narta beach, part of the protected Vjosa–Narta landscape. According to [Reporter.al](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reporter.al), protesters carried banners defending the natural environment and opposing what they described as the seizure of local property by business interests linked to the government.[^cite_note-BIRN-Investors-54]

On 26 May, activists and supporters gathered outside the [Ministry of Environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Environment_\(Albania\)) in [Tirana](31075.md), demanding an immediate halt to the works in Pishë Poro–Narta, publication of the relevant documentation and intervention by the responsible state institutions.[^cite_note-PPNEA-26May-protest-104][^cite_note-OBCT-26May-protest-105]

On 30 May, demonstrators gathered near the project site in Portonovo. During the protest, confrontations occurred between protesters and private security personnel present at the site.[^cite_note-Boldnews-106] Videos and photographs published by Albanian activists and journalists showed physical altercations and the use of pepper spray, which reportedly affected police officers and [Lëvizja Bashkë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%ABvizja_Bashk%C3%AB) activist and journalist Loreta Koleci.[^cite_note-Reporter-31][^cite_note-Boldnews-106] Following the incident, Albanian authorities announced investigations into the conduct of those involved.[^cite_note-107]

The [Albanian Helsinki Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Helsinki_Committee?action=edit\&redlink=1) later stated that, according to its monitoring, the protest had begun peacefully and escalated after a local resident participating in the demonstration was forcibly dragged inside the fenced construction area by civilians who were not part of the contracted Myrto private-security structure. The committee called for a full investigation into the use of force by unauthorized persons and into reports that pepper spray had affected both protesters and police officers.[^cite_note-Helsinki-30]

On 31 May, following the violence at Zvërnec, the protests expanded substantially in Tirana and began their sustained daily phase. Demonstrators marched from the State Police Directorate to the Ministry of Interior and then to the prime minister's office, calling for accountability over the Zvërnec violence and using slogans including "Albania is not for sale" and "cancel the project".[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109]

### June 2026

On 1–2 June, daily demonstrations continued in Tirana against the planned resort project. Protesters gathered in [Skanderbeg Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg_Square) and marched toward the prime minister's office, opposing construction in protected areas, demanding transparency over the development contracts and increasingly calling for the resignation of Prime Minister [Edi Rama](277805.md).[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-NYT-111][^cite_note-AlJazeera-112] Organized groups refused Rama's offer to meet a 20-person delegation, choosing instead to continue public demonstrations.[^cite_note-NYT-111]

From 1 June onward, the movement maintained a daily evening demonstration in Tirana, generally beginning with a gathering in [Skanderbeg Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg_Square), followed by speeches outside the prime minister's office and a march through central streets. The demonstrations initially began at 18\:00; from 17 June, the start time was moved to 19\:00 because of high temperatures.[^cite_note-Lapsi-15June-evening-schedule-113][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-17June-schedule-change-114]

At the same time on 1 June, the Albanian Coalition for the Family and Life held a separate *March for Life* from Skanderbeg Square to the Ministry of Health, opposing a ministry order concerning hormone treatment for transgender patients in the public health system and calling for its withdrawal pending broader consultation.[^cite_note-BoldNews-1June-hormone-protest-115][^cite_note-Newsmax-1June-hormone-protest-116]



Protesters in [Tirana](31075.md) on 2 June

On 3 June, the protests escalated in Tirana. Demonstrators demanded Rama's resignation, criticized opposition leader [Sali Berisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sali_Berisha) and called for the repeal of changes to the law on protected areas.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118] The [Associated Press](18935732.md) reported that demonstrators broke through a police cordon and that police used water cannon to disperse the crowd.[^cite_note-AP-3June-90] Protesters also stopped outside the [Democratic Party of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Albania) headquarters, criticizing the party's position on the project and Berisha's earlier support for it.[^cite_note-Opozitë_e_Shitur-119] A small number of anti-Israel messages appeared during the early demonstrations; international and regional sources described these as conspiracy narratives and not representative of the movement's main demands.[^cite_note-politico.eu-88][^cite_note-Euronews-Hybrid-120][^cite_note-BIEPAG-Flamingo-4]

On 4–9 June, the Tirana demonstrations continued and the movement spread to other Albanian cities, including [Korçë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kor%C3%A7%C3%AB), [Fier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fier), [Kukës](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuk%C3%ABs), [Vlorë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlor%C3%AB), [Elbasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elbasan), [Durrës](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durr%C3%ABs), [Shkodër](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shkod%C3%ABr), [Pogradec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogradec), [Gjirokastër](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gjirokast%C3%ABr) and [Lezhë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lezh%C3%AB).[^cite_note-Shqiptarja-5June-cities-121][^cite_note-Shqiptarja-7June-cities-122] On 6 June, a separate gathering was held at Dalan beach in the Zvërnec area, returning the demonstrations to the coast near the proposed development.[^cite_note-Ora-Dalan-6June-123] On 8 June, demonstrators gathered below [Rozafa Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rozafa_Castle) during Rama's visit to Shkodër, opposing the developments at both Zvërnec and Rrjoll.[^cite_note-Shqiptarja-Shkoder-Rama-8June-124] On 9 June, protesters marched through central Lezhë and raised broader concerns about living costs, unemployment and emigration, while smaller gatherings continued in Elbasan and Gjirokastër.[^cite_note-ReportTV-Lezhe-9June-125][^cite_note-TopChannel-regions-9June-126] Reuters and Al Jazeera reported that several thousand people continued to protest in Tirana while holding flamingo symbols and calling for the project to stop.[^cite_note-Reuters-5June-91][^cite_note-127]



Protesters in Tirana on 7 June

On 8 June, tensions were reported inside the protest movement after members of the [Youth Forum of the Democratic Party of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_Forum_of_the_Democratic_Party_of_Albania) attempted to join the Tirana demonstration. Some protesters opposed their participation and accused the Democratic Party of trying to take over or divide the movement, while organizers later limited public speeches to selected representatives from fields such as science and education.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-dq-129]

On 10 June, the protest in Tirana was organized as a larger nationwide gathering and coincided with the 148th anniversary of the [League of Prizren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Prizren). Hundreds of thousands of people participated in the protest.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-10June-crowd-24] Protesters used the slogan "Prizren 1878, Tirana 2026", while media and commentators described the movement as having broadened from opposition to the Zvërnec project into a wider challenge to the political system, the main parties and the Rama government.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-Reuters-2026-06-10-3][^cite_note-131]

On 13 June, residents of [Rrjoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rrjolli) protested against a separate resort construction project, broke through part of the fencing around the site and entered the construction area, with tensions reported between residents, police and private security.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-Rrjoll-2026-06-14-11][^cite_note-132] Later the same day, the Tirana protest became one of the largest gatherings of the movement, with strong participation from the Albanian diaspora. International media estimated the crowd at 100,000 to 200,000 participants, while protesters continued demanding Rama's resignation and action against corruption across the political class.[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-diaspora-list-2026-06-13-135]



Protesters in Tirana on 13 June

On 14 June, protesters marched from Skanderbeg Square to the prime minister's office and later onto the [Tirana–Durrës Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SH_2_\(Albania\)), blocking the highway for several hours before heading toward the Rinas road and [Tirana International Airport Nënë Tereza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirana_International_Airport_N%C3%ABn%C3%AB_Tereza).[^cite_note-rr-136][^cite_note-137] Around 02\:00 on 15 June, police units, including [Shqiponjat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shqiponjat_\(police_unit\)) and [Rapid Intervention Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Intervention_Force_\(Albania\)) (FNSH), stopped the march near the airport. Protesters sat on the Rinas road for about an hour and later returned toward Tirana, dispersing in the early morning.[^cite_note-138]



Protesters in Tirana on 14 June

Between 15 and 19 June, daily evening protests and marches continued in Tirana. Protesters repeatedly targeted both the government and the older opposition, while police later opened criminal proceedings against 19 people over the Rinas march and against 16 people after protesters entered the Fan Zone in Skanderbeg Square during World Cup broadcasts.[^cite_note-pp-42][^cite_note-fz-43] On 18 June, protesters marched to the [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Parliament) building during a plenary session, called on deputies to resign and confronted Socialist Party MP [Ardit Bido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ardit_Bido), who was escorted away by police.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

On 20 June, the protest became the largest demonstration of the movement to date. It was organized as a nationwide anti-government mobilization in Tirana, with participants arriving from across Albania and from the [Albanian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_diaspora).[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142] The crowd filled much of [Dëshmorët e Kombit Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%ABshmor%C3%ABt_e_Kombit_Boulevard), while speakers presented the movement as a peaceful national civic revolt against corruption, state capture and the old political class.[^cite_note-143] Albanian media estimates placed participation at more than 250,000 people.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-2026-06-21-250k-28]



Protesters in Tirana on 21 June

On 21–23 June, the protest wave broadened to other civic and social issues. In [Kakome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakome_Bay), residents and emigrants protested over long-running property disputes and removed sections of fencing around disputed land; the protest was reported as peaceful.[^cite_note-144] On 22 June, the protest coordinating group presented a five-point platform calling for Rama's resignation, a 12-month non-partisan technical government, constitutional and electoral reforms, term limits for the office of prime minister, repeal of disputed laws and development packages, and a new social contract.[^cite_note-Lapsi-2026-06-22-demands-14][^cite_note-Syri-2026-06-22-demands-15] On 23 June, the [Union of Trade Unions of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_unions_in_Albania) held a related protest in Tirana over wages, staffing levels, social dialogue, trade-union rights and the quality of public services.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146]

On 25–26 June, protesters again read the five-point platform and sent an official letter to Western ambassadors and heads of mission accredited in Albania. The letter described the movement as peaceful and civic and asked diplomats to take the protesters' non-negotiable demands seriously.[^cite_note-Shqiptarja-25June-147][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-ambassadors-26June-148] A small group of protesters also began spending the night near the prime minister's office, a practice that continued intermittently in the following days.[^cite_note-Shqiptarja-overnight-26June-149]

On 27 June, another nationwide Saturday gathering was held with significant diaspora participation. Italian MEP [Ilaria Salis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilaria_Salis) joined the protest and described the movement as an example of civic courage in Europe.[^cite_note-Lapsi-27June-28th-150][^cite_note-151] On 28 June, the demonstrations entered their fifth week, and protesters announced a larger nationwide mobilization for 4 July.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-29th-28June-152][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-4July-28June-153]

On 29 June, the movement marked one month of daily demonstrations. Several European political representatives joined or addressed the rally, including MEPs [Tineke Strik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tineke_Strik), [Daniel Freund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Freund), [Cristina Guarda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cristina_Guarda), [Jutta Paulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jutta_Paulus) and [Anna Strolenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Strolenberg), while later coverage also reported speeches by [Nicolae Ștefănuță](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolae_%C8%98tef%C4%83nu%C8%9B%C4%83), a vice-president of the [European Parliament](9581.md), and [Vula Tsetsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vula_Tsetsi), co-chair of the [European Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Green_Party). Their remarks focused on democracy, anti-corruption, environmental protection and Albania's European future.[^cite_note-Dosja-29June-MEPs-154][^cite_note-Pamfleti-29June-live-155][^cite_note-Bowing_2026-156]

On 30 June, protesters gathered outside the [Parliament of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Albania) during a plenary session. Demonstrators accused the Parliament of failing to represent citizens and demanded the resignation of Rama and his government. The protest included symbolic actions against deputies and official vehicles, and tensions increased after eggs were thrown at Socialist officials and vehicles.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-30June-Parliament-2Hours-157][^cite_note-158] Several protesters were escorted to police stations after physical confrontations with police. [Opportunity Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opportunity_Party_\(Albania\)) leader [Agron Shehaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agron_Shehaj) and MP [Erald Kapri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erald_Kapri) went to the Tirana Police Directorate and later to police stations to seek information about those taken by police; according to Shehaj, six protesters had been escorted or detained. BalkanWeb later reported that the six had been released after several hours.[^cite_note-Pamfleti-6Detained-159][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-6Released-37]

Later that evening, the regular protest resumed in Skanderbeg Square and outside the prime minister's office. Protesters again marched through Tirana, targeting both the government and the old opposition, and announced a gathering outside [SPAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Structure_against_Corruption_and_Organized_Crime) for the following day.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161]

### July 2026

Throughout July, the regular 19\:00 demonstrations continued daily in [Tirana](31075.md), generally beginning in [Skanderbeg Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg_Square), proceeding to the prime minister's office and then through central streets.[^cite_note-165]

On 1 July, protesters gathered outside [SPAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Structure_against_Corruption_and_Organized_Crime) in Tirana and submitted material related to the book *The Albanian Files*, which they described as evidence of state capture, unlawful development in protected areas and conflicts of interest. Activist Sidorela Vatnikaj entered the building to submit the material, while lawyers connected to the protest movement said they were preparing a criminal complaint related to the issues raised by the publication.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] The regular evening protest later continued in Tirana, where protesters again demanded Rama's resignation, investigations into Rama and deputy prime minister [Belinda Balluku](65301641.md), and a protest outside Parliament the following day.[^cite_note-169]

On 2 July, protesters gathered outside the [Parliament of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Albania) during a plenary session, in the most serious escalation of the movement up to that point. Police surrounded the Parliament area and deployed additional security measures, including an armoured vehicle and a water tanker, while protesters threw eggs and flour at Socialist Party deputies and vehicles and called on deputies to resign.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173] The protest escalated after demonstrators attempted to remove part of the metal barrier and breached a police cordon. Police used tear gas, pepper spray and water cannon after protesters threw rocks, eggs, bottles and other objects.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175][^cite_note-Agolli_2026-7]

After the clashes, 19 people received medical treatment, including 15 police officers and four protesters or citizens. More than 20 protesters were escorted or detained, while several lawyers alleged excessive force, irregular procedures and mistreatment in custody. Reports and videos also showed police entering a nearby café and removing several protesters, including activist Gent Progni, who later alleged from inside a cell that he had been beaten while handcuffed.[^cite_note-Clashes_2026-32][^cite_note-Escorted_2026-38][^cite_note-Panorama_2026-176][^cite_note-177][^cite_note-Lawyer_2026-178] Later that day, protesters gathered outside the prime minister's office and then marched to police station no. 3 on Mine Peza Street, demanding the release of detained protesters.[^cite_note-179]

On 3 July, protesters gathered in Tirana. The gathering began in Skanderbeg Square before moving toward the prime minister's office, police station no. 3 and the Tirana Prosecutor's Office, with Rama's resignation and the release of those detained after the 2 July clashes remaining central demands. Organizers presented the protest as a prelude to a larger nationwide mobilization announced for 4 July.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183]



Protesters in Tirana on 4 July

On 4 July, a nationwide and diaspora mobilization was held in Tirana. Hundreds of thousands of people participated in the protest.[^cite_note-Syri-4July-crowd-29] Earlier in the day, heavy traffic and long queues were reported at border crossings, including at Morinë, where more than 11,000 citizens entered Albania within four hours. A Kosovo Border Management Center notice said queues had formed because Albanian authorities were registering people entering Albanian territory, while diaspora groups also entered through Muriqan and other crossings.[^cite_note-Lapsi-border-delays-184][^cite_note-CNN_2026-185][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]

The 4 July protest began in [Skanderbeg Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg_Square) and continued along [Dëshmorët e Kombit Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%ABshmor%C3%ABt_e_Kombit_Boulevard) toward the prime minister's office. Protesters carried national flags, placards and anti-government symbols, and continued demanding Rama's resignation, a technical government, investigations by SPAK and the release of protesters detained after the 2 July Parliament clashes. The protest also included symbolic actions tied to Rama's birthday, including a mock birthday cake and a symbolic coffin. A tall bust of Rama was also toppled with a rope, in an action that the [Associated Press](18935732.md) compared to the 1991 toppling of the statue of communist leader [Enver Hoxha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enver_Hoxha).[^cite_note-AP-4July-symbolism-188][^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191] After several hours outside the prime minister's office, protesters marched to police station no. 3 on Mine Peza Street. Tensions occurred there after windows were broken at the building, and police used water to disperse the crowd.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-Lapsi-police-station-damage-36]

Also on 4 July, lawyer Gentian Serjani alleged that detained protester Niko Qarkaj had been beaten by police while being held in the security block near Mine Peza Street after suffering epileptic seizures. Serjani said Qarkaj had shown him bloodied clothes, had not received proper assistance during the seizures and had been on hunger strike since his detention. Qarkaj was later taken by ambulance from the security block to hospital, while his lawyer said the People's Advocate and the [Albanian Helsinki Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Helsinki_Committee?action=edit\&redlink=1) had been notified.[^cite_note-Qarkaj_2026-9][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194]

On 5 July, residents of [Nikaj-Mërtur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikaj-M%C3%ABrtur_region) in [Tropojë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropoj%C3%AB) and environmental activists protested against a [KESH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KESH) project for the construction of three dams on the Mërtur River, arguing that the works lacked adequate consultation and threatened the area's natural and tourism value.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196] The same day, the Tirana court reviewed security measures for 21 people detained after the 2 July Parliament clashes, placing two protesters, Everest Bardhi and Ervin Gega, under house arrest, ordering reporting obligations for 14 others and releasing five without a security measure.[^cite_note-Lapsi_2026-197][^cite_note-198][^cite_note-Zeri-5July-security-measures-39]

Between 5 and 10 July, daily protests continued in Tirana, with demonstrators maintaining demands for Rama's resignation, a transitional government and accountability over the 2 July police response and detentions.[^cite_note-205]

During the same period, protesters also reacted to the government's decision to allocate 400 million lek (€4.2 million) from the state budget's reserve fund for a [Kanye West](523032.md) [concert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ye_Live_Concert_Tour) near Tirana. The allocation was approved through a normative act on 6 July, using the constitutional procedure for urgent and necessary measures.[^cite_note-Reporter-Kanye-funding-2026-07-07-206] Reuters reported that the decision added to public anger amid the protests, while Rama defended the funding as necessary to prevent the event's cancellation and argued that it would generate at least €100 million in revenue. At the 9 July protest, activists denounced the concert funding alongside police action over online reviews of businesses.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208]

Opposition groups subsequently pursued legal challenges to the allocation. On 10 July, [Lëvizja Shqipëria Bëhet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%ABvizja_Shqip%C3%ABria_B%C3%ABhet) submitted a request to the [Constitutional Court](29207004.md) seeking the annulment of the normative act.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-ShqiperiaBehet-Kanye-Constitutional-209] On 13 July, the parliamentary group of the [Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Albania) filed a criminal complaint with [SPAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Structure_against_Corruption_and_Organized_Crime) against Rama and culture minister [Blendi Gonxhja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blendi_Gonxhja). The complaint alleged abuse of office and violation of equality in public procurement in connection with the allocation; the allegations had not been adjudicated.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-PD-SPAK-Kanye-13July-210]

On 11 July, another nationwide and diaspora mobilization was held in Tirana. Protesters gathered in [Skanderbeg Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg_Square) and marched toward the prime minister's office, while organizers said members of the [Albanian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_diaspora) would again join the demonstration. BalkanWeb reported that protesters continued to demand Rama's resignation and marched through several main streets of Tirana, with later drone footage showing tens of thousands of participants in the capital's streets.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212]

Between 12 and 19 July, daily protests were held in Tirana. Demonstrators gathered in [Skanderbeg Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg_Square), continued to the prime minister's office and marched through central Tirana, maintaining their demands for Rama's resignation.[^cite_note-222] On 13 July, lawyer and activist [Altin Goxhaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altin_Goxhaj?action=edit\&redlink=1) also filed a criminal complaint with [SPAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Structure_against_Corruption_and_Organized_Crime) against State Police director [Skënder Hita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sk%C3%ABnder_Hita?action=edit\&redlink=1), alleging abuse of office and unlawful persecution in connection with police inquiries into negative online reviews of businesses; the allegations had not been adjudicated.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-Hita-SPAK-13July-223]

On 20 July, a protest was held outside the [Parliament of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Albania) during a plenary sitting. Protesters gathered near the side and main entrances, where additional police units and metal barriers had been deployed, and repeated demands for Rama's resignation and investigations into Rama and [Belinda Balluku](65301641.md).[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-20July-Parliament-224][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-20July-51st-225] Some protesters carried eggs marked with the names of government officials and deputies and threw eggs at legislators' vehicles; MP [Vangjel Dule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vangjel_Dule) was also targeted as he entered Parliament.[^cite_note-Syri-20July-eggs-226][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-20July-Dule-227] A larger protest outside Parliament remained announced for 23 July, coinciding with the final plenary sitting before the summer recess.[^cite_note-Syri-23July-announcement-228]

On 21 July, residents of [Prrenjas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prrenjas) held a protest against a proposed territorial reform that they said would merge the municipality with [Librazhd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Librazhd). Protesters argued that the change would weaken local self-government and the area's cultural and educational identity, and warned that demonstrations could escalate if the plan proceeded.[^cite_note-Lapsi-Perrenjas-21July-229]

On 22 July, protesters gathered in Tirana. After speeches outside the prime minister's office, demonstrators marched through the city's main streets, repeated their demand for Rama's resignation and made a symbolic stop outside the [Parliament of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Albania). Protesters called for a further gathering outside Parliament on the morning of 23 July, during the final plenary sitting before the summer recess.[^cite_note-Syri-22July-53rd-230][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-22July-53rd-231]

On 23 July, a protest was held outside the [Parliament of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Albania) during the final plenary sitting before the summer recess. Protesters gathered around the approaches to the building, repeated demands for Rama's resignation and denounced Parliament as unrepresentative. Police deployed metal barriers, riot units and a water cannon. Tensions escalated when demonstrators rattled or attempted to cross the barriers and threw eggs and bottles toward police, lawmakers and official vehicles; police responded with water cannon, tear gas and pepper spray.[^cite_note-Reuters-23July-Parliament-232][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-23July-Parliament-233][^cite_note-Syri-23July-police-response-234]

After the sitting ended, groups of protesters remained near Parliament and threw eggs at several ministers, Socialist Party deputies and official vehicles. Seventeen people received medical treatment at [QSUT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_Teresa_Hospital_\(Tirana\)) and the University Trauma Hospital, all of whom were reported to be outside life-threatening danger, while at least 11 people were taken to police stations.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-23July-Lamallari-235][^cite_note-Lapsi-23July-injured-33][^cite_note-Panorama-23July-detentions-40] Despite the morning confrontation, the regular 19\:00 demonstration proceeded as usual, beginning in Skanderbeg Square and continuing to the prime minister's office and through central Tirana.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-23July-evening-protest-236]

From 24 to 26 July, the evening protests followed the movement's established route from Skanderbeg Square to the prime minister's office and through central Tirana, with demonstrators maintaining demands for Rama's resignation and broader political change.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-24July-55th-237][^cite_note-Lapsi-25July-56th-238][^cite_note-Lapsi-26July-57th-239] On 25 July, the march continued to police station no. 3, where protesters demanded the release of people detained following the 23 July Parliament protest. Organizers also announced a further demonstration outside Parliament on 27 July, a call repeated during the 26 July rally.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-25July-police-station-240][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-26July-Parliament-call-241]

On 27 July, a protest began at 08\:30 outside the [Parliament of Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Albania) during a plenary sitting. Police established a wider security perimeter, closed adjoining roads and positioned water-cannon vehicles near the building. Protesters moved between several approaches to Parliament and briefly blocked nearby streets before marching past the Ministry of Interior and along Elbasan Street and returning to the parliamentary area.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-27July-Parliament-242][^cite_note-Lapsi-27July-security-243] Tensions arose when demonstrators attempted to cross a police cordon and threw eggs toward officers; police used pepper spray and detained five people, including coordinating-group member Arben Kola. One protester was taken to hospital after exposure to the spray.[^cite_note-Lapsi-27July-detentions-244][^cite_note-Panorama-27July-hospital-34] The demonstration later moved to police station no. 1 to demand the detainees' release; all five were subsequently released. When the sitting resumed in the afternoon, seven additional protesters were detained, and eggs were thrown at the vehicle of Socialist parliamentary-group leader [Taulant Balla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taulant_Balla) after he left the building.[^cite_note-Panorama-27July-station-245][^cite_note-Syri-27July-detentions-41][^cite_note-Syri-27July-Balla-246]

On 30 July, residents of [Poliçan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poli%C3%A7an) protested against the [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_of_Albania)'s proposed territorial reform, which would remove Poliçan's status as a separate municipality. Demonstrators argued that the change would contribute to depopulation and demanded that the municipality be retained. Participants signed a petition and burned Socialist Party membership cards and a copy of the territorial-reform draft. Erion Zylyftari, the chair of the party's local branch, publicly announced his resignation during the protest, describing the proposal as unjust.[^cite_note-Syri-30July-Polican-247][^cite_note-BalkanWeb-30July-Polican-248] The regular evening protest also continued in Tirana, where demonstrators marched through central streets, returned to [Dëshmorët e Kombit Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%ABshmor%C3%ABt_e_Kombit_Boulevard) and repeated calls for Rama's resignation and criticism of the wider political class.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-30July-Tirana-249]

On 31 July, protests against the [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_of_Albania)'s proposed administrative-territorial map were held in [Dimal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimal_\(municipality\)), [Divjakë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divjak%C3%AB), [Klos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klos,_Albania) and [Prrenjas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prrenjas). In Dimal, residents gathered on the town's pedestrian street, opposed a proposed merger with [Berat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berat) and signed a petition demanding the retention of separate municipal status, citing the municipality's population, agricultural economy, territorial extent and historical identity. Residents of Divjakë rejected a proposed merger with [Lushnjë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lushnj%C3%AB), while mayor Josif Gorrea said that he felt betrayed by the proposal. In Klos, residents opposed the municipality's proposed dissolution and merger with [Mat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mat_\(municipality\)), and municipal employees boycotted work for several hours; protesters also criticized the absence of broad public consultation. Residents of Prrenjas renewed their opposition to a proposed merger with [Librazhd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Librazhd).[^cite_note-Lapsi-31July-municipalities-250] The regular evening protest also continued in Tirana, beginning in [Skanderbeg Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skanderbeg_Square) and proceeding to the prime minister's office before a march through central streets. Demonstrators maintained demands for Rama's resignation, a technical government and constitutional changes, while speakers expressed support for the municipalities opposing the territorial reform.[^cite_note-Lapsi-31July-Tirana-251][^cite_note-Lapsi-31July-messages-252]

### August 2026



Protesters in Tirana on 1 August

Throughout August, the regular 19\:00 demonstrations continued daily in [Tirana](31075.md) on the same general route, with Rama's resignation remaining the principal demand.[^cite_note-257]

On 3 August, residents and local-government representatives in [Patos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patos,_Albania) protested outside the municipal building against a proposal to dissolve the municipality and merge it with [Fier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fier). Demonstrators used the slogan "Hands off Patos", argued that the merger would weaken the city's identity and contribute to depopulation, and called for consultation with the local community before any change to its administrative status. Protesters also emphasized Patos's historical development around the oil industry. Mayor Fation Duro, a [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_of_Albania) member, opposed the proposal, arguing that Patos ranked highly in the national Municipal Functionality Index and was the most efficient municipality in [Fier County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fier_County).[^cite_note-Lapsi-3August-Patos-residents-258][^cite_note-Lapsi-3August-Patos-local-government-259]

On 5 August, residents of [Divjakë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divjak%C3%AB) continued protesting against the proposed dissolution of the municipality under the government's territorial-reform plan. Demonstrators argued that a merger would reduce access to local services and adversely affect the area's economic and social life. They called for broader participation by residents and municipal employees, marched through the town and said that the demonstrations would continue until the proposal to dissolve the municipality was withdrawn.[^cite_note-Lapsi-5August-Divjake-260]

On 7 August, residents of [Memaliaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memaliaj) protested against a proposal to dissolve the municipality and merge it with [Tepelenë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tepelen%C3%AB). Demonstrators called for Memaliaj to retain its municipal status and local institutions, arguing that a merger would make public services less accessible, particularly for residents of outlying areas, and criticizing what they described as insufficient public consultation over the territorial reform. Protesters also emphasized the town's distinct identity and its historical association with mining, and said that demonstrations would continue against the proposed merger.[^cite_note-Lapsi-7August-Memaliaj-261]

On 9 August, during the Tirana demonstration, a group of protesters marched from [Dëshmorët e Kombit Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%ABshmor%C3%ABt_e_Kombit_Boulevard) to the nearby embassies of [Serbia](29265.md) and [Ukraine](31750.md). The action followed Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md)'s visit to Belgrade, where, alongside Serbian president [Aleksandar Vučić](13349267.md), he reiterated Ukraine's non-recognition of [Kosovo](17391.md) and stated that Kyiv's position on Kosovo's 2008 declaration of independence remained unchanged, while emphasizing respect for Serbia's territorial integrity.[^cite_note-EWB-Zelenskyy-Kosovo-2026-262] The remarks drew criticism in Kosovo, whose institutions had condemned Russia's full-scale invasion on 24 February 2022, declared support for Ukraine's sovereignty and territorial integrity and aligned with sanctions against Russia.[^cite_note-Kosovo-Presidency-Ukraine-2022-263] Serbia, while condemning the invasion and supporting Ukraine's territorial integrity, had continued to refuse sanctions against Russia and maintained longstanding political and economic ties with Moscow.[^cite_note-Reuters-Serbia-Ukraine-8August-264] Protesters symbolically referred to the street as "Free Dardania" instead of the official "Free Ukraine", and chanted in support of the [Kosovo Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_Liberation_Army).[^cite_note-Lapsi-9August-embassies-265]

On 10 August, residents of [Mirakë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirak%C3%AB?action=edit\&redlink=1), in [Librazhd Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Librazhd), protested over access restrictions they attributed to construction work on [Corridor VIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-European_Corridor_VIII). Residents said that road works had left seven villages in Librazhd Municipality and two in [Elbasan Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elbasan) with access only in the Elbasan–Librazhd direction, while the suspension of intercity transport services had further limited mobility for people without private vehicles. Protesters called for an immediate solution to restore access.[^cite_note-Lapsi-10August-Mirake-266]

Also on 10 August, residents of [Orikum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orikum) gathered outside the local administrative centre, calling for Orikum to be restored as a separate municipality rather than remain part of [Vlorë Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlor%C3%AB). They also opposed the proposed new administrative-territorial reform and called for the views of the local community to be taken into account.[^cite_note-Lapsi-10August-Orikum-267]

On 19 August, residents of [Patos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patos,_Albania) held a second protest against the proposed merger with [Fier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fier), warning that the change could reduce local services and lead to the closure or downsizing of municipal institutions.[^cite_note-Lapsi-19August-Patos-268]

## International protests



Rally by the [Albanian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanians_in_Germany) in [Berlin](3354.md) on 21 June

Several solidarity rallies were announced and later held among [Albanian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_diaspora) communities in Europe, North America and Australia, while additional protest calls circulated for other cities.[^cite_note-269] Early international rallies and protest calls included Berlin, Munich and Stockholm on 5 June; London, Milan, Florence, New York City, Washington, D.C., and Toronto on 6 June; Brussels, Bologna and Skopje on 7 June; and a further New York gathering announced for 15 June.[^cite_note-AlbaniaNotForSale-international-270][^cite_note-dc-271]

On 7 June, diaspora protests were also held in several cities including London, Brussels, Hamburg, Milan, New York City and Bern, with participants opposing the government's handling of the planned developments and calling for greater transparency and accountability.[^cite_note-dp-272] Further simultaneous rallies were planned for 14 June in 17 cities across Europe and North America, including [New York City](645042.md), [Chicago](6886.md), [Boston](24437894.md), [Michigan](18859.md), [Toronto](64646.md), [Berlin](3354.md), [Munich](19058.md), [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne), [Stuttgart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuttgart), [Athens](1216.md), [Genoa](47332321.md), [Faenza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faenza), [The Hague](30269.md), [Strasbourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strasbourg), [Vienna](55866.md), [Oslo](22309.md), and [Odense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odense).[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-diaspora-list-2026-06-13-135][^cite_note-273]

A civic movement involving a coordinated road trip of Albanian expatriates travelling from London to Tirana to join national protests occurred in June.[^cite_note-274] Although the journey lasted a few days, the goal was to be in Tirana for the organized protests on 20 June. The convoy began voyaging with 45 automobiles from [London](17867.md). Later on, more automobiles joined the motorcade from [Belgium](3343.md), [Germany](11867.md), [Austria](26964606.md), [Hungary](13275.md) and other European countries, and then entered Albanian territory near [Shkodër](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shkod%C3%ABr).[^cite_note-275] After arriving in Tirana, the motorcade arrived in [Laprakë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laprak%C3%AB), and then its participants marched on foot to the Prime Minister's office.

### Europe



Albanian protesters outside of the Albanian embassy in [London](17867.md), 7 June 2026

On 6 June, Albanians in [Vienna](55866.md) held a protest against the Albanian government, carrying placards such as "Albania is not for sale" and chanting for Rama to resign.[^cite_note-276] On 7 June, people gathered near the headquarters of the [European Union](9317.md) in [Brussels](3708.md) in support of the protests in [Albania](738.md), carrying [Albanian flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Albania) and placards calling for environmental protection and transparency over the planned development.[^cite_note-277] On 8 June, during a protest organized by Andrey Ralev in [Sofia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sofia), demonstrators called upon the [European Union](9317.md) to act immediately and side with the protesters in Albania.[^cite_note-Protest_in_Sofia-278] On 13 June, Albanians gathered in [Lyon](8638634.md) with flags and flamingo symbols in support of the protests in Albania.[^cite_note-Me_flamingo_dhe_flamuj_në_duar-279]

On 5 June, several hundred demonstrators gathered near the Embassy of Albania in [Berlin](3354.md) to protest against the project, while also raising concerns about pensions, working conditions, education, and government policies affecting young people.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281] On the same day, another rally was held near the Albanian Consulate in [Munich](19058.md), with the call "For nature, for justice, for Albania".[^cite_note-hashtag-diaspora-282] On 7 June, diaspora protests were also held in [Hamburg](13467.md), [Stuttgart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuttgart) and [Heidelberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heidelberg).[^cite_note-dp-272][^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] On 13 June, dozens of Albanians gathered in [Nuremberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg) as part of the diaspora protests.[^cite_note-Me_flamingo_dhe_flamuj_në_duar-279] On 14 June, Albanians held another protest in Munich alongside the demonstrations in Tirana, chanting "Rama in prison, Berisha in prison".[^cite_note-285] On the same day, several hundred people participated in a demonstration in [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne).[^cite_note-286]

On 16 June, Rama was confronted by Albanian protesters in Berlin during his visit to the Annual Conference of the Eastern Committee. As he left the event, protesters chanted slogans including "Edi Rama, traitor, resign as soon as possible", "Shame", "Cancel the project" and "Cancel the law"; Rama waved at the protesters before entering his car.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288]

On 1 July, Rama was again met by Albanian diaspora protesters in Germany, this time in [Düsseldorf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%BCsseldorf), where he had travelled for a meeting with [Hendrik Wüst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hendrik_W%C3%BCst), the [Minister-President of North Rhine-Westphalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister-President_of_North_Rhine-Westphalia). A group of Albanian protesters carrying national symbols gathered at the start of the meeting and called for Rama's resignation. BalkanWeb reported that the protesters also raised placards and slogans against laws which, according to them, favoured the privatization of public assets, linking the Düsseldorf protest with the wider demonstrations taking place in Tirana.[^cite_note-289]

On 6 June, Albanian migrants and solidarity activists demonstrated in [Syntagma Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syntagma_Square), [Athens](1216.md), opposing the planned tourism development near the protected [Vjosa–Narta wetlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vjosa%E2%80%93Nart%C3%AB_Protected_Landscape?action=edit\&redlink=1) and the Zvërnec coastline. Participants carried banners and flamingo symbols associated with the movement.[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291] On 13 June, people gathered in [Dublin](8504.md) to protest against Rama's government and demand his resignation.[^cite_note-Syri-Dublin-2026-06-13-292]

On 6 June, around 2,000 people gathered in [Milan](36511.md) to join the protests, holding signs, singing national songs and shouting slogans against the Albanian government.[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294] On the same day, 450 people gathered in [Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence) for a similar protest.[^cite_note-295] On 7 June, hundreds of Albanians gathered in [Bologna](21069333.md), chanting slogans against [Edi Rama](277805.md) and opposing the planned resort project.[^cite_note-296] On 14 June, diaspora rallies continued in Italy. [Genoa](47332321.md) and [Faenza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faenza) were listed among the planned 14 June gatherings, while protests were also held in [Rome](25458.md) and [Pisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pisa), where participants carried Albanian flags and placards and called for the resignation of the Albanian government.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-diaspora-list-2026-06-13-135][^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298]

On 4 June, activists and citizens gathered outside the Embassy of Albania in [Pristina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pristina) in solidarity with the protests in Albania. They opposed planned interventions in protected natural areas and criticized what they described as the privatization of public spaces.[^cite_note-299] On 7 June, Albanians protested in [The Hague](30269.md) against the Albanian government and the planned development in Zvërnec, using slogans including "Albania is not for sale".[^cite_note-300] On 7 June, a solidarity protest was listed in [Skopje](29618.md), at Macedonia Square, as part of the international protest calendar connected with the movement.[^cite_note-AlbaniaNotForSale-international-270]

On 5 June, a protest was held in [Stockholm](26741.md) with the call "Save Vjosa-Narta".[^cite_note-hashtag-diaspora-282] On 13 June, the Albanian diaspora in Stockholm again protested, chanting "Rama resign" and "Rama in prison" and accusing the Albanian government of selling the country.[^cite_note-Syri-Stockholm-2026-06-13-301] On 7 June, people gathered in [Bern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bern) as part of wider diaspora protests held in several European and North American cities.[^cite_note-dp-272] On 13 June, another protest was held outside the Embassy of Albania in Bern, with participants calling for Rama's resignation.[^cite_note-302]

On 6 June, members of the [Albanian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanians_in_the_United_Kingdom) gathered outside the Embassy of Albania in [London](17867.md) to protest against the planned resort development and call for Rama's resignation.[^cite_note-Rama_jep_dorëheqjen!-303] The protest continued the following day, when Albanians living in the United Kingdom gathered again outside the embassy with national flags and placards calling for environmental protection, transparency and Rama's resignation.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305] On 13 June, Albanians in London again gathered with flamingo symbols and Albanian flags as part of the wider diaspora mobilization.[^cite_note-Me_flamingo_dhe_flamuj_në_duar-279]

### North America



Rally by the [Albanian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Canadians) in [Toronto](64646.md) on 6 June

On 6 June, people in [Toronto](64646.md) took part in the protests, wearing red and black clothing and carrying Albanian flags while calling for Prime Minister [Edi Rama](277805.md) to resign.[^cite_note-306] Toronto was also listed among the cities where further diaspora rallies were planned for 14 June.[^cite_note-BalkanWeb-diaspora-list-2026-06-13-135]

On 6 June, an estimated 1,500 to 2,000 people gathered outside the Albanian Consulate in [New York City](645042.md) in support of protecting the Vjosa-Narta wetlands and opposing the planned resort project.
